#2760. What we learned from the Spurs win over the Warriors

I was about 12 years old when I realized that there were people in the Dark Ages who had lived amongst the ruins of the Roman Empire.

Once a week in my art class (usually on a Friday) we were given free reign to pursue whatever we wanted, so long as it pertained to either the creation or study of art.

Being a poor visual artist, I tended to opt for study, and would plop myself into a beanbag chair in the corner of the room with a stack of art books.

In the time before the proliferation of google image searches this felt like a revelation, and I spent countless hours pouring through volumes of paintings that I'd otherwise only have seen in a museum.

I can still recall the goosebumpy thrill of stumbling into the deeply shadowed chiaroscuro of El Greco, or the eerie moonlit majesty of John Atkinson Grimshaw.

But it was a piece by the French Romantic painter Hubert Robert, found in a compendium of work contained in the Philadelphia Museum of Art, that so confounded me that I begged my teacher to make me a photocopy of it.

I kept my humble paper copy of 'Ruins of a Roman Bath with Washerwomen' in a manila folder on my childhood bookshelf for many years.

From time to time I would take it out and just stare at it. I couldn't understand why it had captured me so, but I knew it had something to do with the nature of the situation.

If you've seen it, you know that it depicts a towering Roman edifice surrounding a once-ornate bathhouse. It's clear that the architecture is no longer being maintained. The age is visible. There are stones missing.

And yet, it's still so majestic. The ruin itself testifies to the grandeur that came before.

The structure is still enormous and strong, and I suspect that one of the first thoughts that anyone has looking at it, is to wonder what it must have looked like at the peak of the empire.

If it were just a painting of those ruins, it would be arresting enough as is.

Gathered around the edges of the previously opulent pool, are hunched over figures, doing their laundry in the remains of the bathhouse.

I had developed quite a real fondness for the subject of history by the age of twelve, but this was something that had never occurred to me.

People had continued living in Roman ruins.

I suppose some part of my brain had to have registered this, knowing that the Colosseum was still standing, but to me, ruins weren't a place where people lived.

Sure, if they were important enough, maybe someone would build a museum, and people would come to visit them, but the thought of anyone actually living there was astonishing.

But people had in fact done that. It's all over the historical record. They did their best to maintain the plumbing and the roads, and the structures, and just stayed where they were.

Crusaders patched and fortified them. Some people carried on as if the Western Empire still existed, awiating a return to glory that never came.

Last night I felt that familiar feeling washing over me any time I saw Warriors fans in the crowd, as the Spurs punctured any illusions of a lingering Golden State greatness.

It's one thing to witness the fall of an empire. It's another thing altogether to keep living there.

For a moment (and in spite of Draymond Green's flagrant antics), I felt a wave of pity wash over me. It quickly turned to empathy with the realization that I too had been there.

Thirty points down at the half, with an all-too-clear vision of the future in front of them, these people were still holding on to a return to glory. Maybe not in this game, but to the season as a whole.

Steph Curry turns 39 in March. Draymond Green hasn't been an offensive plus in years. Jimmy Butler is still to return from an ACL injury at an age where recovery is negligible.

Promising rookie Yaxel Lendeborg was learning hard lessons about facing the defense of true contenders (he went 1-13), and probably won't be up to speed in the way that aging squad needs as they approach their 40's.

There's no young Kawhi Leonard to tie everything together. The sun is setting. Has, realistically, already set.

There are still people doing their laundry in the ruins.

And who am I to blame them? If not for the arrival of a certain French sensation, I'd probably be right there with them.

But it does speak to the difference in approaches from the two franchises.

Pursuing longevity, the Popovich/Duncan-era Spurs almost never cashed in all their chips for an all-or-nothing swing at a title. They kept building as they went. And when the end of their run was well and truly visible, they looked at what they had left and tried to repurpose it in a productive way.

It's not terribly dissimilar from what some of those old Roman communities did.

Recognizing the end for what it was, the citizens of cities like Avneches, Aosta, and Split began to build their own structures within the confines of the old city walls, utilizing some of the remaining architecture as ready-made stone quarries for their rebuilding efforts, while retaining the meticulously laid-out street grids of their predecessors.

There seems to be this idea circulating around the NBA that a rebuild must tear everything down to the absolute studs. That nothing should remain of the previous era. No notion. No mentality. No prolonged eye on the future.

But that's not how the Celtics or Lakers did it. And it's not how the Spurs have done it, either.

Sometimes it's okay to acknowledge the wisdom of a previous era, while knowing that it's time to turn the page. Not everything needs to be abandoned. There are some points of expertise that endure throughout the ages.

But you can't just live in the ruins, either, hoping for a lottery ticket to cure all ills, or a glorious return to form.

These Spurs aren't the Spurs of old, and that's important to acknowledge.

They're building something of their own now, but they're doing so within the thoughtfully constructed confines of a previous glory, and the result is something that feels both new and familiar.

Something as fresh as it is reminiscent. Something as aspirational as it is nostalgic.

You can't just do your laundry in the halls of a fallen kingdom. That's how you end up losing your shirt.

I have to say, Harrison Barnes has looked surprisingly spry so far this season. I assumed he would function as emergency depth when he was re-signed, but he look more like the early-season version of himself from last season, before a slump that allowed Julian Champagnie to work his way into the starting lineup. While he's certainly not a starter anymore, he's savvy and sharp-shooting enough to serve as the hot hand on nights where Carter Bryant is missing shots and assignment. That probably raises some questions about Bryant at the same time, but it really underscores just how much functional depth the Spurs have right now.

It's also been great seeing Luke Kornet find more of rhythm with the bench unit, after a somewhat concerning opening slate. There's no way the Spurs win in such a landslide without him stifling their 2nd unit, and that was his 3rd out of the last 4 games with a positive plus/minus, putting him back into the positive in Defensive Wins Shares and Defensive Box Plus/Minus for the season. I still have concerns about the postseason, but it's nice to see the trend reverse as the Spurs have badly needed it to.

And while I'm not sure that Stephon Castle's outstanding performance is indicative of how the rest of the season is going to go for him, it was an absolute blast of adrenaline to see him shoot the lights out against the Warriors specifically, especially after seeming hesitant over the past stretch of games, with the additional pain of his shot not falling. Still, you can really see why the Spurs are committed to turning him into an off-ball threat, because when that shot is falling, you become almost impossible to handle. Kudos to Mitch for having the good sense to ride the hot hand on a night when Wemby was getting a lot of defensive attention, but it also felt like this would have been a blowout even if Wemby had ridden the pine after the 1st quarter. If this was Castle finding his shot and his confidence, they really might keep trucking on at a 68-win pace. That quite literally changes everything, and bring into question whether or not rotations should even be altered all that much once Tobias Harris makes his debut, barring situational need.

Also, I'd just like to note that Keldon Johnson has really become such a patient jack-of-all for the team. It really doesn't matter who you put him with, he's going to find the point of need and do his best to fill it. There have been night where his scoring isn't needed/prioritized, and he just spent his time crashing the boards and giving it his all on defense, and nights where he comes alive on the offensive end when a particularly juicy matchup presents itself, or the team just needs more points. I'm not sure how the Spurs are going to re-sign him with their eyes on the cap and a whole host of contract balancing issues to come, but I'd love to see them do it. He's not a perfect player by any means, but he's really become a perfect Spur.

#2759. Broncos have mastered the art of winning close games

The Denver Broncos know how to win games.

No matter the situation this team is in, the Broncos never lose belief that they're still in the game. That's one of the main reasons Denver has set the NFL record for 16 straight wins in one-score games.

It's also why the bye week comes at a great time. The Broncos are eager to use it for the stretch run, but also to give everyone a breather from these heart-racing finishes.

As Denver head coach Sean Payton said to the media after the win over the Carolina Panthers in one-score games:

"Well, part of it pisses me off because there's been a few of them that you would say shouldn't have been one-score games, and then obviously there's some that are, but they've been great. We knew coming in Thursday, we just knew the type of team we were playing. They've been doing some really explosive things on offense. They were banged up, certainly, on defense, as we were.

"But we'll take this break, but we still have to be — and it's not that you're never satisfied, but we still have some work to do. To your question, I don't think I have a great answer. I think we had a decent start. We ended up settling for a field goal."

For the Broncos, that confidence you're never out of a game is a powerful thing. It's not so much easier for the team, but a belief.

"I'll be honest, it seems like it's always the same feeling," Broncos quarterback Bo Nix said to the media after the win over the Panthers. "There's always a look in the huddle that, like, hey guys, we don't have a choice right here. We got to go make something out of it. Today, those second-half touchdowns were really, really important. We were down by one there, 14-13, and we needed one and went and scored, missed the two-point, and got another opportunity. Then, (we) went down and got another touchdown.

"I thought that was the best part of the game because it showed that we stacked two drives together, two critical moments, and gave our defense some rest on the sideline with those longer drives. Also, a good cushion, and they did just a good job responding and scoring."

Since it keeps happening, is it lucky or good?

"Well, when it keeps happening, I think our league is what it is," Payton said to the media after the win over Carolina. "When those things keep falling your way, that's not by accident."

#2758. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2757. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2756. Dodgers notes: Jonathan Loaisiga, Tim Hill, Shohei Ohtani

The 2027 Dodgers will indeed look different compared to this year's squad, as right-hander Evan Phillips returned to his old stomping grounds in Atlanta.

Now with several voids in the bullpen, the Dodgers have begun inquiring on a pair of former Yankees relievers, those being right-hander Jonathan Loaisiga and left-hander Tim Hill. The Dodgers are one of five teams interested in Loaisiga and are one of three teams interested in Hill. Jesse Rogers of ESPN and Jon Heyman of the New York Post tweeted the team's interest in Loaisiga and Hill respectively.

After spending parts of eight seasons with the Yankees, Loaisiga spent the 2026 season with the Arizona Diamondbacks, posting a 2.42 ERA and a 2.88 FIP while striking out 48 and walking 13 across 67 innings. Hill is coming off his worst season in pinstripes, as he posted a 4.60 ERA and a career-worst 5.93 FIP, striking out just 25 and walking 13 across 58 2/3 innings.

The biggest detriment for the Dodgers this past season was the injury bug, most notably the pair of injuries that plagued Shohei Ohtani both on the mound and at the plate. The Dodgers are the only team that has to worry about maintaining one of their key starting pitcher's health while also ensuring that same pitcher continues to lead the team in home runs hit.

The top priority for both Ohtani and the team entering 2027, as noted by Sonja Chen of MLB.com, is fulfilling the tough demand of having the two-way superstar healthy as a pitcher and hitter for the entire season.

Having a healthier Ohtani may not have changed the Dodgers' fate this past season, and they can't let themselves get caught up in wondering what could have been. But finding a way to get Ohtani through a two-way season at full strength, or close, could be as valuable as any transaction the team may make this offseason.

Dylan Hernandez of the California Post reported that the Dodgers sent a three-man envoy to Yokohama High School to scout teenage right-hander Shoki Oda.

Former Dodgers outfielder Trayce Thompson will be reuniting with his older brother Klay in Miami, as the younger Thompson will join the Marlins' baseball operations department, reports Christina De Nicola of MLB.com.

#2754. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2753. Victor Wembanyama, Spurs surge in Post’s NBA Power Rankings

Each week, the California Post will power rank the NBA's 30 teams. Records are as of Saturday morning:

The reigning Western Conference champions remain the hottest team in the league after blowing out the Steph Curry-less Warriors on Friday night. The Spurs have won 11 games in a row. They're top three offensively and defensively across the league, all while Victor Wembanyama is playing at an MVP-caliber level. (Last week, 3rd)

Victor Wembanyama #1 of the San Antonio Spurs looks on during a game against the Golden State Warriors in San Antonio, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Luka Dončić may have cooled off from his two-week-long 40-point barrage, but the Slovenian superstar continues to lead the Lakers in exceeding early-season expectations. The Lakers have looked like a potential championship team after seven consecutive wins. And the team received encouraging news regarding Austin Reaves' heel injury, which the Lakers have classified as a bruise. (Last week, 2nd)

The Celtics made a statement with their midweek blowout victory over the 76ers. They're elite on both ends of the floor, and Paul George has been an impeccable fit next to Jayson Tatum. (Last week, 8th)

The defending champions were looking like they were turning a corner on their season, having won four straight games, before back-to-back losses to the Cavaliers and Pacers. They should be fine in the long run, but they need better play from Mikal Bridges and Josh Hart. (Last week, 14th)

Cooper Flagg continues to shine in his second NBA season, dropping 43 points, 10 rebounds and six assists in Monday's win over the Pacers. And Kyrie Irving (21.6 points per game) looks like his pre-ACL injury self. (Last week, 17th)

The Heat are finding their groove after starting the season 0-4. Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo are finding ways to better coexist, combining for 74 points in Friday's win over the Cavaliers. (Last week, 19th)

Meleek Thomas (13 points on 49% shooting, 47.3% on 3-pointers) has been a revelation for the Cavaliers, who acquired the guard out of Arkansas with the No. 34 pick in June's draft. #2751. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

The full list of holdings and current market values is available from CNBC.com's Berkshire Hathaway Portfolio Tracker.

-- Alex Crippen, Editor, Warren Buffett Watch

#2750. Juice your growth and save on taxes in this workplace investment account

Workers are in the heart of open enrollment season for next year's benefits, and rising healthcare costs are shining a spotlight on an offering that includes a tax-advantaged investment account.

Healthcare expenses are climbing for workers and employers alike, with average total family premiums, including the employer's share, averaging $28,303, up 5% from a year ago, according to data from health policy organization KFF, formerly the Kaiser Family Foundation.

To manage the costs, a growing number of employers are moving to high-deductible health plans, which work alongside health savings accounts or HSAs. Almost 40% of companies with at least 10 employees that offer health benefits also have an HSA-qualified plan, KFF found.

Health savings accounts have three major tax advantages. First, contributions that your employer automatically deducts from your pay aren't taxed. Second, money stashed into the HSA grows free of taxes. Finally, you can make tax-free withdrawals for qualified health expenses – just be sure to save your receipts.

"I love HSAs because of their triple-tax advantage," said Eileen Olson, CFP and founder of Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina, near Savannah, Georgia. "For people who can afford to leave it alone, it can be one of the best retirement accounts they're going to have."

In 2027, those with individual healthcare coverage through a high-deductible plan can stash up to $4,500 in an HSA, including any contributions from employers. That number goes up to $9,000 for family plans. Individuals can kick in a catch-up contribution of $1,000 if they're aged 55 and older and not enrolled in Medicare.

The HSA differs from the healthcare flexible spending account, known as the FSA.

FSA funds must be used by the end of the plan year unless your employer allows a carryover of up to $700 or an additional 2.5 months to use the money. HSAs, however, have no expiration date on their funds – and that's what makes them such a powerful tool for long-term planning. You can also take the HSA with you if you change employers.

"Get the money deployed, take advantage of the pretax contribution, and allocate the money into a growth portfolio," said Mark Stancato, certified financial planner at VIP Wealth Advisors in Decatur, Georgia. "With the HSA, you can keep compounding year over year."

The structure of an HSA and the available investment menu will vary from one plan to the next, and some will require that employees keep some of their holdings in cash.

Preset HSA fund menus tend to have 15 to 25 funds to choose from, and providers say that accountholders who invest tend to use two to four funds, according to Greg Carlson, senior research analyst for equity strategies at Morningstar.

How you allocate your HSA will depend on several factors, including your family's health prospects in the next year, your cash flow needs – how much you can comfortably sock into the HSA – and your risk tolerance, said André Small, CFP and founder of A Small Investment in Houston.

Small's standard is to keep an amount equal to 120% of the plan's out-of-pocket maximum held in cash in the HSA.

"That covers a bad year without forcing a sale at the wrong time," he said. "Above that amount? I typically advise investing the amount the client is comfortable investing, allocated to their risk tolerance, their time horizon and the goal of spending these dollars on care later."

For 2027, the IRS defines a high-deductible plan as one with an annual deductible no less than $1,750 for self-only coverage ($3,500 for family plans) and with an out-of-pocket expense limit that doesn't exceed $8,700 for self-only (or $17,400 for family coverage). Out-of-pocket expenses include deductibles, copays and other costs – but not premiums.

Other advisors suggest having up to the deductible in cash, though some HSA plan providers require a stated minimum amount in cash.

Beyond this cash reserve, financial advisors recommend allocating into stocks.

Patrick Swift, CFP and president of Amplius Wealth Advisors in Blue Bell, Pennsylvania, aims for an allocation of up to 60% in U.S. large cap stocks, 20% in small and mid-cap stocks and 20% in international stocks.

"We believe the equity portfolio should be well diversified," he said. "If the [HSA] balance is 2% to 10% of your net worth, why not be aggressive? That can change based on the situation, but if you are comfortable with the philosophical aspect, why not be growth oriented?"

Similarly, Stancato likes to go heavy on equity exposure in HSA accounts. "I allocate more dollars into small caps and emerging markets – those asset classes with more volatility but also more opportunity for growth," the advisor said. He also adds some large-cap domestic exposure.

For the adventurous, select HSA providers are offering brokerage window access, meaning investors can pick from a broader array of investments, including individual stocks.

This year, seven providers surveyed by Morningstar offer access to brokerage windows – up from four in 2023 – including HealthEquity, Fidelity and Lively. Be aware that there may be annual fees attached, either a flat dollar amount or a percentage of assets.

Rachel Elson, a CFP and wealth advisor at Perigon Wealth Management in San Francisco, recommends keeping things simple. "You can do a three-ETF model, covering some allocation that includes the U.S. market, and two ETFs for developed and emerging markets."

"You don't need to get super complicated here," she added.

#2749. Sunday Patriots Notes: 10 things we learned in Munich

The New England Patriots are in the German city of Munich this week, and set to take on the Detroit Lions for the sixth international game in franchise history.

The team of head coach Mike Vrabel made the trip on Thursday, and so did a select group of media representatives — including from Pats Pulpit. Today's edition of our Sunday Patriots Notes will therefore look a bit different, and not just serve as a wrap-up of the week that was but rather a look at the trip and its host city.

1. The Patriots have a balance to strike: All work and no play makes the Patriot a dull boy — it's not quite as dramatic but flying to a different country to play a regular season game is obviously a difference experience for the players involved. For some of them, it is the first trip abroad in their lives meaning that they had to get passports and experience the challenges of transatlantic travel (more on that in a second).

At the end of the day, it is still a work trip for the team and one that is being taken seriously. The Patriots, who are coming off a 45-27 loss against the Packers, have made that very clear: they want to bounce back from last week's defeat and head into their bye week as the owners of a 6-4 record.

(On a site note: the Lions do not get the benefit of a bye after the Germany trip; they already had theirs in Week 6).

That said, they also did not spend the entire four-day journey in the team hotel either. For starters, players had practice and media obligations, and they were given some free time in the afternoons. Some of that was used on getting food — McDonalds received rave reviews from players such as center Jared Wilson because it offers chicken wings in Germany — while some participated in a guided tour of the city.

2. Travel is a challenge: Brenden Schooler is not a stranger to flying to Europe. This week's trip is the third in his five-year career after he already played in Frankfurt in 2023 and in London in 2024, two games the Patriots ended up losing. And yet even for a veteran such as himself travel is a challenge.

The Patriots left from Providence on Thursday evening with the goal of getting some sleep in on the flight. The body still has to adjust to the six-hour time difference, and for Schooler that showed the following night: he woke up at 2:30 a.m. and before getting back to sleep hit the workout room in the team hotel to get a brief session in. Other players shared similar experiences.

Getting those disruptions out of their way sooner rather than later was the plan behind an earlier-than-usual trip. The Patriots held a walkthrough practice in Munich on Thursday followed by a regular session and another walkthrough on Friday and Saturday, respectively.

The Lions, meanwhile, took a different approach: they traveled 24 hours later, meaning their adjustment schedule is theoretically behind the Patriots'. And yet, head coach Dan Campbell is "very confident" in his decision.

"We have got up and moved around, and I know that every eight hours here, we're going to begin to acclimate," he said, via Pride of Detroit's Jeremy Reisman. "I know by the time we hit Sunday morning into that Sunday afternoon game, we're going to be ready to go. So, we will be primed and ready, and I feel very good about our plan."

3. Players have learned their lessons from prior international trips: Like Brenden Schooler, DeMario Douglas has also been part of the Patriots' previous two journeys to Europe. And for him, one of the main goals this week was simply to "eat well" — something he apparently did not do in both Frankfurt and London.

One of the reasons for that, however, might have been his own culinary choices: he went to a Five Guys the last time the Patriots played an international game. Lessons learned, one would assume.

4. Lorenz Metz is in the spotlight: Unless they are some big-name players on the back-nine of their careers, practice squadders enjoy a relatively anonymous life. Chances are that, until this week, you haven't heard Lorenz Metz's name mentioned often if at all whether you live in Germany or the United States.

That all changed this week. Metz, who grew up an hour east of Munich, was made one of the faces of the operation. On Thursday, he took the podium alongside the likes of Drake Maye and Christian Gonzalez and drew considerable interest especially from the local media in the audience (in an unexpected twist, he also opted to answer all questions be they in English or German in his native language).

Metz, who also organized and paid the traditional O-line dinner on Thursday night, will step out of the spotlight soon; carrying an international exception, he is ineligible to play on Sunday. For a few days, however, he was — at least in the eyes of the German media present — one of the headline names.

5. Getting to know Germany was on the to-do list: Yes, the Patriots made it very clear this was a work trip. However, they also mixed together work and fun in order to get familiar with their home country — both in their free time and in the meeting room: as Mike Vrabel noted, the team did a quiz asking players to name Germany's neighboring countries.

We didn't get full results, but safety Craig Woodson said that his teammate Lorenz Metz managed to name "a couple" of the nine countries in question.

6. At least temporarily, Munich is all-NFL: Walking around the old town in Munich this week, you are confronted with a fascinating dichotomy. On the one hand, you see workers setting up Christmas markets and getting the city ready for the coming weeks. On the other hand, you see the NFL represented on every corner. Storefronts, flags, thousands in NFL gear — pro football has managed to take over a city typically dominated by soccer.

The city itself also is actively trying to promote itself along the way, unsurprisingly. As part of the efforts, media members from both New England and Detroit were given a tour of the city including a dinner that can best be described as plentiful.

Unsolicited tip: go to the new city hall and take the elevator up the tower for some of the best views of the city and, when the weather is right, the Alps.

7. Paranoia is a constant: It's no secret that NFL teams are paranoid; they operate behind closed doors and making sure not to spill any of the tricks of their particular trade. That paranoia, it turns out, travels. Practicing at the FC Bayern Munich training facility in a southern suburb, the Patriots did not leave anything up to chance — to a point where they installed temporary walls to shield some of the view of the practice fields.

8. A pro-Lions crowd? When arriving in Munich on Thursday, you could the impression you made the trip to Detroit: from the people on the street to the atmosphere in the different team pubs, this was a definitive pro-Lions crowd. Part of it has to do with the contest being designated a Lions home game, and the city therefore hosting more events catered toward that particular fan base (there was, for example, a big Lions fan fest). It also is the first Lions international game since 2015, and first ever in Germany.

However, the balance of crowd power has since shifted. The main reason for that simply seems to be the composition of the fan bases themselves, with the Patriots having more fans in Europe who don't need to turn the trip into a four-day weekend.

The Patriots also had another ace up their sleeves: the Tartan Army, Scottish soccer fans who were invited to visit the game by team owner Robert Kraft after they spent parts of the summer in Boston to support their country at the world cup. Now, they are in Munich supporting the Patriots.

9. FC Bayern is king, but… The Patriots have been closely cooperating with the most successful German football team, FC Bayern, for multiple years now. This cooperation resulted in the team practicing at the club's facility at the Säbener Straße in southern Munich — with players such as Serge Gnabry and Luis Diaz, wearing Patriots jerseys, of course, watching practice from the field level.

Bayern are not just well-represented in an NFL context, but all over the city. Walking through the old town, you see multiple team-affiliated shops selling merchandise, and those were all popular tourist attractions as well.

However, FC Bayern is not the only team in the city. In fact, it's other club, which is the older of the two, enjoys wide-spread support: 1860 Munich, a one-time German champion now playing fourth-team soccer. They actually played a game on Saturday, drawing 0-0 against Unterhaching. 1860's nickname, by the way? The Lions.

10. You get to see plenty of NFL jerseys: Before wrapping this up, a quick roundup of Patriots jerseys that have been spotted over the last few days: Drake Maye, Christian Gonzalez, Hunter Henry, Tom Brady, Julian Edelman, Rob Gronkowski, Devin McCourty, Mac Jones, Jakob Johnson, Sebastian Vollmer, A.J. Brown, Gino Cappelletti and John Hannah.

With the crowd not only divided between the Patriots and Lions, however, there also are a lot of other teams and players represented. The most random one spotted? That would be a Brock Osweiler Broncos jersey.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2748. While tied for NBA's best record, Lakers believe they can be better

While rocketing toward the top of the league, the Lakers have been far from perfect. That's good news to them.

"A big thing that we talk with our players about a lot is being able to learn and get better and try to correct things even when you are winning," coach JJ Redick said.

The Lakers (11-2) have won seven consecutive games entering Sunday's 1 p.m. PST matinee against the Suns (5-6) in Phoenix.

Using a rare multiple-day break between games to audit their performances, coaches and analytics staffers showed slide presentations to players about both offensive and defensive flaws this week. The lectures left the team with one of the best records in the league feeling as if it was one of the worst, forward Ziaire Williams joked.

Already the top-ranked offense in the league, the Lakers should strive to match that by becoming a top-10 defense, Redick implored his team. Williams is not stopping there.

"Top 10 should be at least like our standard, if not better," the forward said. "Our half-court defense has been great. Obviously, rebounding has been one of the biggest things for us, cleaning up transition as well. But the potential's there. We have the size, the length, the athleticism. It's just about going out there and executing."

Read more: Lakers' Austin Reaves considered day-to-day with heel injury

The Lakers, ranked 18th in defensive efficiency, thrive on forcing turnovers and protecting the rim. Their 18.5 turnovers forced per game rank third in the league, and they average an NBA-best 11 steals per game. Center Walker Kessler's 2.2 blocks per game rank third.

But they can't finish the strong half-court possessions. The Lakers are ranked 28th in defensive rebounding and 25th in second-chance points given up.

The Lakers staff rewatched every offensive rebound given up this season. Lack of boxing out was the biggest fault. Then there were instances when players stood and watched.

"If I stand and watch and try and outjump someone else, I'm probably not going to get the rebound," said forward Jake LaRavia, who has been an offensive rebounding revelation since entering the starting lineup, averaging 2.8 offensive rebounds in the last nine games. "So you have to go and be physical and hit bodies, and then just be able to dive on loose balls, finish possessions, it's really just who's going to work harder."

Kessler is averaging 8.1 defensive rebounds during the Lakers' seven-game winning streak — which is the second-longest in Redick's coaching tenure — compared to 6.3 during the first six games.

Forward Jarred Vanderbilt, who did not play in the first four games, has made the most of his limited minutes off the bench, grabbing 7.9 defensive rebounds per 36 minutes, the second-best rate on the team among players who have played more than three minutes. Vanderbilt, averaging 3.8 rebounds per game in 11.9 minutes, had 10 rebounds in less than 14 minutes against the Detroit Pistons on Nov. 8.

Transition defense also is sinking the Lakers' defensive efforts. Opponents are scoring 20.2 points off turnovers per game, which put the Lakers 24th out of 30 teams. Redick blames some of that on the Lakers' offensive flaws. Although they are the league's most efficient offense, the Lakers turn the ball over 16.9 times per game, the fifth-most in the NBA.

Lakers guard Luka Doncic shoots over two Bucks during a win Wednesday in Milwaukee. (Aaron Gash / Associated Press)

Luka Doncic, the league's leading scorer at 38.6 points per game, also averages an NBA-worst five turnovers. Austin Reaves, who is day-to-day because of a left heel bruise suffered Wednesday against Milwaukee, averages 24.7 points and 3.4 turnovers.

Doncic is expected to play Sunday despite tweaking his right ankle late in Wednesday's win. Reaves didn't participate in practice Saturday but Collin Sexton was a full participant. The guard wore a thick Band-Aid over his right eyebrow and had bruising around his right eye after bumping heads with Milwaukee's Ryan Rollins but said he doesn't expect to wear a mask. He needs only to keep the cut covered to ensure it doesn't open up again. The rugged guard is familiar with the protocols; Sexton said this was the 11th time in his career that he's received stitches in his face.

The short break offered an important opportunity for players to rest after a grueling three-game Eastern Conference trip. They now enter a softer stretch in the schedule. After Sunday's game in Phoenix, the Lakers play their next four at home against three teams — Charlotte, Sacramento and Utah, twice — that have a combined six wins.

Their next game against a team with a winning record isn't until Nov. 27 at San Antonio. The 11-2 Spurs, who have won 11 consecutive games, are the only team with an active winning streak longer than the Lakers'.

#2747. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long term

Major indexes were volatile this week due to earnings from artificial intelligence companies, key economic releases, and the scandal involving Nvidia former CEO Jensen Huang. While short-term fluctuations could weigh on investor sentiment, it is prudent to maintain a long-term investment horizon.

Investors looking for attractive long-term picks can track the ratings of top Wall Street analysts and gain key insights from their in-depth research on the macro and micro factors impacting stocks.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company Palo Alto Networks will announce its fiscal first-quarter earnings on Nov. 18. Migration to the cloud and the rapid adoption of AI have increased demand for cybersecurity solutions provided by Palo Alto and its peers. TipRanks' KPIs tool for PANW shows the rise in the company's subscription revenue over the years, driven by the adoption of its AI and security offerings and contributions from recent acquisitions.

Ahead of the upcoming earnings, Oppenheimer analyst Ittai Kidron reiterated a buy rating on Palo Alto stock with a price target of $450. The five-star analyst said that his checks indicate upside potential from CyberArk and Chronosphere acquisitions and strength in the company's core businesses.

Specifically, Kidron expects Palo Alto's Q1 FY27 next-generation security annualized recurring revenue to be about two percentage points above the midpoint of the guidance range and consensus, or $9.55 billion. He also sees nearly 1 to 1.5 percentage points of revenue upside compared to the guidance and consensus of $3.305 billion. Kidron expects management to remain conservative with its FY27 guidance and raise it by no more than the size of the quarterly beat.

Additionally, Kidron highlighted that partner checks indicated above-plan performance in the quarter, driven by firewall-attached subscriptions and strength in Prisma SASE and XSIAM offerings. The analyst also expects results to gain from CyberArk cross-selling and better-than-expected performance of Chronosphere as OpenAI completes its migration.

"While Prisma AIRS crossed $100M in ARR last quarter, the fastest product ramp in company history, partners still view CY27 as the first real revenue year," said Kidron.

Kidron ranks No. 638 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.5%. See Palo Alto Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Next on this week's list is database software company MongoDB. Recently, BTIG analyst Gray Powell initiated coverage of MDB stock with a buy rating and a price target of $417. Powell estimates that currently the size of the company's core addressable market is nearly $15 billion, with several tailwinds to drive more than 20% revenue growth over the next few years.

Beyond its core document and vector businesses, Powell highlighted that MongoDB has exposure to about $72 billion of adjacent markets, projected to grow to about $121 billion over four years. With even modest share gains in adjacent markets, the five-star analyst believes that MongoDB can grow revenue at about a 24% annual rate over the next three years, above management's growth target of more than 20%.

Contrary to the bearish narrative, Powell views AI as an emerging tailwind for MongoDB, with application modernization creating new workloads while making migrations faster and cheaper. He added that interviews with eight contacts were generally constructive and indicated faster legacy migrations, growing non-relational adoption, and early traction in Voyage AI and Vector Search offerings.

Additionally, Powell sees upside to Atlas growth, driven by new products. "We see a meaningful product cycle led by MongoDB 9.0 and AMP, with Agent Engine and Atlas Infinite adding upside over time," said the analyst.

Powell ranks No. 480 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13%. See MongoDB Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Membership-only warehouse chain Costco Wholesale recently announced a special cash dividend of $30 per share, payable on Dec. 4. The company also announced that its board has reauthorized a stock repurchase program of up to $4 billion, which will expire in November 2030 and replaces the current $4 billion program that is set to end in January 2027.

Following the announcement, Oppenheimer analyst Rupesh Parikh reiterated a buy rating on COST stock with a price target of $1,160. The five-star analyst said that the special dividend news is one of the key catalysts that he has been waiting for since Costco was added to Oppenheimer's top pick list in November 2025.

Parikh highlighted that the special dividend the company announced is significantly above his estimate of about $20 per share. The analyst expects the special dividend to reduce interest income substantially.

"COST remains a top pick for us, and we still see the potential for a stock split down the road, which could represent another positive catalyst for shares, in our view," said Parikh.

Overall, Parikh remains bullish on Costco's long-term prospects, backed by its value proposition, leading competitive position, and a solid track record of shareholder returns. The analyst also noted the company's strong management team and the ability to sustain top- and bottom-line growth even in a competitive retail backdrop.

Parikh ranks No. 324 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.7%. See Costco Statistics on TipRanks.

#2746. Position Grades: Minnesota

Tim is out today, so it's a substitute teacher for this week's grades. The good news is that Penn State behaved very well in its 42-14 win against Minnesota. So, I don't have to leave a nasty note about any position groups.

Rocco Becht threw three touchdowns passes and avoided any catastrophic mistakes. He also ran for a score. Alex Manske threw for a score, too. Overall, I'm boosting this grade a bit because with the conditions as they were, it wasn't an easy night to throw and the quarterbacks were better than fine.

Carson Hansen ran for more than 120 yards on 10 carries, showing the explosiveness that he didn't the first half of the season. Hopefully the injury that kept him off the field in the second half heals quickly. Cam Wallace was good in a secondary role, too. His tough fourth down run early in the third quarter will be an underrated play in a 28-point win, while he also caught a touchdown pass. Lastly, James Peoples scored on a wheel route and a good wheel route is worth some bonus points.

The weather. The game script. Those things meant that the wide receivers were hardly targeted at all. Chase Sowell nearly caught another long pass if not for a great play by a Minnesota defensive back. Brett Eskildsen caught the one ball thrown to him and it was the same with Amarion Jackson.

Ben Brahmer only had two catches, but he made them count. His used his large frame to grab a couple of touchdowns and nearly had a spectacular back-of-the-end-zone grab in the second quarter. Andrew Rappleyea had four catches, including one where he may have been as open as any Penn State pass catcher ever.

This group was trending to A++ through the first two and a half quarters, but then Minnesota made some adjustments. Early on, there were massive running lanes for Wallace and Hansen to run through. Meanwhile, check out the pocket that Becht had on his first touchdown throw. It's clear this group has gotten healthy and gotten comfortable playing with one another over the course of the season.

The Gophers had just 41 yards rushing. Meanwhile, there were some disruptive plays from this group. Alexander McPherson had two tackles for loss, including a sack. Dallas Vakalahi had a sack, while Yvan Kemajou opened the game with a beautiful tackle for loss.

Kooper Ebel had his best game in a Penn State uniform, leading the team in tackles and coming up with an early fumble recovery. It's great to see Cam Smith back and healthy – he was active and had a great special teams play, too. The only thing keeping them from an A was it seemed they were attacked a bit late in the third quarter when the Gophers started to make things a little interesting.

Sort of rinse and repeat for the secondary. Overall, Minnesota had less than 200 yards passing on the night. But, they did have those back-to-back scoring drives in the third quarter that came nearly entirely through the air. Therefore, it's hard to give the secondary a full A grade.

Ryan Barker was 5-of-5 on extra points. Nathan Tiyce averaged 44.6 yards per punt and two were downed inside the 20 (thanks Cam Smith for the one). The kickoff coverage was great. Zay Robinson was good with his two punt returns. The lone bad part? There was a costly personal foul penalty on one punt that gave the Gophers better field position ahead of a touchdown drive.

I know there will be a lot of hand wringing about the third quarter – Penn State was outscored 14-7 and it felt like Minnesota was sneaking back in the game. Third quarters have clearly been an issue for the Lions, dating back to the disastrous one against Wisconsin. But, some credit is due to Campbell and staff for how last night's third quarter started. Up 28-0, the Lions stayed aggressive and went for it on a fourth down play with Wallace gaining just enough to earn a first down. That play allowed Penn State to continue on a long drive that ate up nearly half of the quarter and it was a drive that ended with the Lions up 35-0. Even though things stalled from there, Penn State was never truly in danger of losing that game.

It's been a weird year in Minneapolis. Their offense hasn't clicked, but they've played a lot of good teams tough. This game, they dealt with weirdness even getting to Happy Valley – flying in the morning of the game. P.J. Fleck, who I normally find to be hard to listen to, said that was no excuse in seeing Penn State build a four touchdown halftime lead. The Gophers did play hard through the end and, to an extent, made things interesting in the early part of the fourth quarter.

The Denver Broncos can finally take a breath during the 2026 NFL season.

The Broncos are among four teams who have a scheduled bye during Week 10 of the NFL schedule, and they'll emerge from it in a strong position to chase the AFC's No. 1 overall seed coming off last year's AFC Championship game appearance. Denver currently owns a five-game winning streak and sits atop the AFC West standings, despite a point differential of just +12.

After a 26-21 victory over the Carolina Panthers in Week 9, the Broncos are now 6-0 in games decided by less than a touchdown this season. They have wins over the Jaguars, Chiefs and Seahawks that suggest Denver is poised for another deep postseason run.

The Broncos offense has endured rough patches as quarterback Bo Nix returned from the foot injury he suffered in the 2026 playoffs and coach Sean Payton was forced to take back play-calling duties from offensive coordinator Davis Webb. But Denver's defense remains potent thanks to its pass rush and several crucial turnovers have helped fuel the team's success in close games.

#2744. Pistons vs. Heat Discussion: Game Time, TV, Odds, and More

The Detroit Pistons face the Giannis-led Miami Heat for the second time this season, but this is not the same Heat team Detroit beat 128-111 in the second game of the season. The Heat can get above .500 with a win after winning six of its past eight games, including the previous three in a row. The Heat are starting to understand how best to play around Giannis Antetokounmpo, especially Bam Adebayo. In the team's most recent win, Bam and Giannis scored 37 points, and Davion Mitchell was able to support the big two with a 22-point, 17-assist effort.

The Pistons also had Jalen Duren the last time the two teams faced each other, and the big man is still out, and Detroit's big man is still out recovering from a hamstring injury. Detroit's big men have been an issue since Duren went down, and that could be an issue when going against both Bam and Giannis. Of course, the Pistons do have Cade Cunningham, and he has vaulted himself into a top-10 player in the NBA. Cunningham is coming off a career-high 52 points and an NBA-first 50-point, 10-assist, zero-turnover game. I'm not sure what he'll be able to do for an encore, but if the Pistons don't get decent efforts out of John Collins and Paul Reed, it might not matter.

#2742. ‘Absolutely insane!’ - Norway boss reveals truth behind Martin Odegaard's 'terribly tame' display against Wales

Arsenal skipper Martin Odegaard demonstrated why he wears the captain's armband for both club and country during Norway's recent Nations League triumph. Despite appearing uncharacteristically sluggish on the pitch, details have emerged regarding the brutal physical toll the playmaker endured just hours before kick-off.

Norway secured a comfortable 3-1 Nations League victory over Wales, with Antonio Nusa, Andreas Schjelderup, and Erling Haaland all finding the net. However, captain Martin Odegaard lacked his usual spark despite completing the full 90 minutes.

His subdued outing drew public scrutiny from former Norway international Kjetil Rekdal, who questioned the midfielder's energy levels. Manager Stale Solbakken subsequently stepped in to defend his skipper, revealing that Odegaard had been vomiting throughout the night prior to the match.

Speaking to TV 2, Rekdal highlighted how noticeably quiet the Arsenal midfielder looked compared to his usual standards.

"I think he was terribly tame today," Rekdal said. "He looked tired and didn't have the movement he usually has. I think he was much less visible than he usually is."

Solbakken quickly clarified the situation, explaining that Norway's medical and coaching staff had to make allowances for the playmaker before agreeing on a half-time plan to manage his minutes.

"We have had a captain who has been lying around all night and spitting, and who was allowed to take some other liberties than he was supposed to," Solbakken said. "If you lie down all night and throw up, that's it. All credit to him for going the distance. We had a conversation during the break that he should just drive from the start and let us know when the tank was empty."

Inside the Norway dressing room, Odegaard's determination to play through sickness earned widespread admiration from his international colleagues. Defender David Moller Wolfe praised the midfielder's ability to dictate the tempo despite his physical state.

"It's absolutely insane," Wolfe said in the press zone. "He controls the pace of the match the way he wants and the way we've become very accustomed to him doing it. It says a lot about him and how good a player and leader he is."

Second-half substitute Patrick Berg echoed that sentiment, admitting the squad was aware of the difficult night their skipper had endured.

"I understood that Martin had a bad night," Berg said of his team-mate. "It's not exactly optimal when you're going out and playing a football game. It's strong of him to be able to finish."

Having emptied the tank to see out the full 90 minutes against Wales, Odegaard's immediate focus turns to recovering fully from the illness. Both Norway and Arsenal will hope the physical toll of playing while unwell leaves no lasting fatigue as the midfielder continues his duties for club and country.

#2734. Celtics get good injury news ahead of game against Mavericks

BOSTON — The Celtics will have their full roster available when they host the Dallas Mavericks on Sunday night. Boston has no players listed on its injury report — that includes Paul George, who missed Wednesday's win over the Toronto Raptors with an illness on the second night of a back-to-back.

George sat out in Toronto just a day removed from a 36-point performance against the Philadelphia 76ers. He has been the Celtics' second-leading scorer so far this season, averaging 22 points, 5.2 rebounds, 3.8 assists, and 2.1 steals per game. He's also been the team's most efficient high-volume scorer, shooting 48.8% from the field and 44% from three.

Still, the Celtics survived without him on Wednesday, erasing a 22-point deficit on the second night of a road back-to-back to beat the Raptors 98-93. The win capped a perfect 4-0 road trip and extended Boston's winning streak to five games. At 10-2, the Celtics lead the Eastern Conference, have won 10 of their last 11 games, and own the league's best net rating.

Boston has had three days off since returning from Toronto, and now opens a home back-to-back; the Celtics host the Raptors on Sunday and then the Orlando Magic on Monday night.

Sunday's clean report doesn't guarantee the Celtics will be at full strength on both nights. Boston has rested players on back-to-backs before this season: Mitchell Robinson sat the front end in Orlando on Nov. 1, and George sat the second night in Houston the following day.

Jayson Tatum, meanwhile, has played in every back-to-back game so far, though he's said he's open to conversations about rest management. He also noted that he doesn't like sitting out road games, which has factored into his decision to play on the second night of multiple back-to-backs.

"It's my 10th year, so I understand the balance of wanting to play every game, and rest, and back-to-backs, three-in-fours, five-in-sevens," Tatum said last week in Charlotte. "It's always a conversation. We talk about it. We have a plan. I don't really like sitting out on away games, but we just kind of figure it out as we go."

The Celtics will begin a four-game West Coast road trip on Wednesday, which includes stops in Denver, Sacramento, San Francisco, and Washington D.C.

Dallas will arrive in Boston after facing the Cleveland Cavaliers on Saturday night; the Mavericks entered that game at 7-5 after getting blown out by the Minnesota Timberwolves.

Cooper Flagg and Kyrie Irving were both off Dallas' injury report for Saturday's game.

Flagg, the reigning Rookie of the Year, is averaging 24.6 points, 6.9 rebounds, and 6.5 assists per game, while Irving is scoring 21.6 points per game. Irving, who returned this season from a torn ACL, sat out the second night of Dallas' first back-to-back of the season for load management, so it's possible he misses Sunday's game, though the Mavericks have yet to list their injury report.

Max Christie (left ankle sprain), Sergio De Larrea (right knee sprain), Tarik Biberović (right heel bursitis), and Dereck Lively II (right foot surgery recovery) were all ruled out ahead of Saturday's game.

Sunday will be Flagg's second game at TD Garden. The Newport, Maine native drew plenty of support from the crowd during his Garden debut last March; the Celtics won the game by 20 as Jayson Tatum made his return from his Achilles injury.

Celtics-Mavericks tips off on Sunday night at 6pm ET.

#2732. Are the Bears playing today? NFL Week 10 schedule, next game, NFC North standings

Caleb WIlliams threw four interceptions last Sunday night, a career high. The Chicago Bears won anyway.

Cairo Santos bailed them out, hitting a 38-yard field goal as time expired to beat the Tampa Bay Buccaneers 27-24. Chicago turned the ball over five times and still carried a four-game winning streak into its bye week at 7-2, alone in first place in the NFC North.

The Detroit Lions and Minnesota Vikings are a half-game back at 6-2. The Green Bay Packers are a game back at 6-3. The four teams went a combined 25-9 through nine weeks, the best mark for any division at that point since the 1970 merger. The Lions play the New England Patriots in Munich this morning. The Vikings visit the Packers this afternoon. The Bears get to watch.

General manager Ryan Poles did his shopping early. Chicago landed defensive tackle Jeffrey Simmons from the Tennessee Titans in late October. Then it stood pat at Tuesday's trade deadline, even with a cornerback on its wish list.

Williams is the reason the Bears are here. He led the league in QBR, ESPN's all-in-one quarterback rating, before the Tampa Bay mess. Chicago has scored 30 or more points in four of the five games he's finished in coach Ben Johnson's second season.

The run game hasn't kept up. D'Andre Swift is averaging 3.7 yards per carry. Kyle Monangai, who ran for 146 yards against the Jets in Week 4, hasn't played in more than a month because of toe and thumb injuries.

Defensive coordinator Dennis Allen's group lives on third down. Opponents convert 22.8% of the time, the lowest rate in the league. Tampa Bay went 2-for-12.

#2731. ‘Doing all that crying’ – Kendrick Perkins snaps at ‘soft’ Donovan Mitchell’s request for NBA

Kendrick Perkins called out Donovan Mitchell after his request to the NBA to possibly cancel the Cleveland Cavaliers' game against the Dallas Mavericks on Saturday.

The seven-time All-Star's request was led by the fact that there was a nationwide grounding of the flights by the FAA, which resulted in the Cavaliers having to fly on Saturday from Miami, after playing the Heat on Friday.

Eventually, the game went on, but the team decided to rest all their starters against the Mavs. However, Perkins wanted to make his displeasure towards Mitchell clear after his comments to the media.

After facing a tough battle against the Miami Heat, which they lost 133-121, the Cavs had to fly back home to play the Dallas Mavericks.

The unprecedented situation led to more than 4,700 flights being cancelled on Friday night, which meant the Cavs had to stay in South Beach and fly back to Cleveland on game day.

"This is unprecedented. Back-to-back. Three-hour flight. It was already going to be a tough one," told Mitchell as per Chris Fedor.

Perkins had a straightforward response to these comments. He said: "Soft as h***!!! Doing all that crying… leave some room for the babies."

The Cavs ended up winning the game without Mitchell and James Harden, as Tyrese Proctor and Mario Hezonja combined to score 46 points, while Meleek Thomas and Thomas Bryant added 20 and 19 points, respectively, in a 127-117 win for the Cavaliers.
